He continued: “When I struck the second blow, the cities of Caesar and their surroundings were displayed before me until I saw them with my own eyes.” They said, “Messenger of Allah, pray that Allah will grant us their conquest, give us their lands as spoils, and bring about the destruction of their power at our hands.” The Messenger of Allah ﷺ supplicated for that.
He continued: “When I struck the third blow, the cities of Abyssinia and the surrounding villages were displayed before me until I saw them with my own eyes.” The Messenger of Allah ﷺ then said: “Leave the Abyssinians alone as long as they leave you alone, and leave the Turks alone as long as they leave you alone.”
Good: Narrated by al-Nasāʾī (3176), whose wording this is, and Abū Dāwūd (4302).

8923 — ʿĀʾishah reported: The Prophet ﷺ was unable to sleep one night and said: “If only a righteous man from among my Companions would guard me tonight.” We then heard the sound of weapons. He asked: “Who is there?” The man replied, “Saʿd, Messenger of Allah. I have come to guard you.” The Prophet ﷺ then slept until we could hear him breathing deeply in his sleep.
Authentic: Narrated by al-Bukhārī (7231) and Muslim (2410). This wording is al-Bukhārī’s.
In Muslim’s narration, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ asked him: “What brought you here?” He replied, “I became concerned for the safety of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, so I came to guard him.” The Prophet ﷺ prayed for him and then went to sleep.
The Saʿd mentioned here was Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, as explicitly stated in other narrations.

8924 — Jābir ibn ʿAbdullāh reported: On the day of the Confederates, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ asked: “Who will bring us news of the people?” Al-Zubayr said, “I will.” He asked a second time: “Who will bring us news of the people?” Al-Zubayr again said, “I will.” He asked a third time, and al-Zubayr again volunteered. The Prophet ﷺ then said: “Every prophet has a devoted disciple, and my devoted disciple is al-Zubayr.”
Agreed upon: Narrated by al-Bukhārī (4113) and Muslim (2415/48).
The words “Who will bring us news of the people?” refer to obtaining news about Banū Qurayẓah’s breach of their covenant. Ḥudhayfah’s mission, by contrast, was to obtain news about Quraysh and took place during a bitterly cold night.

8925 — ʿAbdullāh ibn Abī Awfā reported: The Messenger of Allah ﷺ supplicated against the Confederates, saying:
“O Allah, Revealer of the Book and Swift in reckoning, defeat the Confederates. O Allah, defeat them and shake them.”
Agreed upon: Narrated by al-Bukhārī (4115) and Muslim (1741/21).

8926 — ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib reported that the Prophet ﷺ said on the day of the Trench:
“May Allah fill their houses and graves with fire, just as they kept us occupied from the middle prayer until the sun set.”
Agreed upon: Narrated by al-Bukhārī (4111) and Muslim (627/202).

8927 — Jābir ibn ʿAbdullāh reported: On the day of the Trench, ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb came after sunset, denouncing the disbelievers of Quraysh. He said, “Messenger of Allah, I was barely able to perform the afternoon prayer before the sun was about to set.” The Prophet ﷺ replied: “By Allah, I have not yet performed it.”
We went down with the Prophet ﷺ to Buṭḥān. He performed ablution for prayer, and we did likewise. He performed the afternoon prayer after sunset, then performed the sunset prayer afterward.
Agreed upon: Narrated by al-Bukhārī (4112) and Muslim (631/209).

8928 — Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī reported: On the day of the Trench, when hardship had reached its limit for us, we asked the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, “Is there anything we should say?” He replied:
“Say: O Allah, conceal our vulnerabilities and calm our fears.”
Allah then defeated the enemy by means of the wind.
Good: Narrated by al-Bazzār in Kashf al-Astār (3119).

8929 — Abū Hurayrah reported that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ would say:
“There is no deity except Allah alone. He strengthened His army, granted victory to His servant, and defeated the Confederates by Himself. Nothing remains after Him.”
Agreed upon: Narrated by al-Bukhārī (4114) and Muslim (2724/77).
The words “He defeated the Confederates by Himself; nothing remains after Him” are an example of praiseworthy rhymed prose. Blameworthy rhymed prose is contrived and forced, whereas praiseworthy rhyming arises naturally and harmoniously.
